Notice is hereby given that the Bluffdale City Council will hold a meeting Wednesday, October 28, 2020, at the Bluffdale City Hall,
2222 West 14400 South, Bluffdale, Utah, scheduled to begin promptly at 4:30 p.m. or as soon thereafter as possible. This meeting will
also be broadcast live to the public at: www.bluffdale.com The public may comment at the meeting or by emailing comments to
councilmeetingcomment@bluffdale.com Notice is further given that access to this meeting by the Mayor and or City Council may be
by electronic means via telephonic conference call.

WORK SESSION 4:30 P.M. – (The work session is for identifying future items and other council discussion. In
accordance with Utah Code § 52-4-201(2)(a), while the meeting may be open to the public, there will not be any
opportunity for public input during the work session).

To:     Mark Reid, City Manager
        Mayor Timothy
        City Council
CC:

RE:     2020 Impact Fees Facility Plan (IFFP)




City Staff is proposing to revise the 2020 Impact Fee Plan (IFFP) to reduce some of the costs
especially as it regards to water, transportation and public safety impact fees. Staff recognized that
some of the fees might be higher than typical in our region and for this reason we have
postponed/reduced some of projects or items to purchase that would be funded through the impact
fees.

With this effort, we are also refining and simplifying the method for calculating transportation impact
fees. Our consultant, Ryan Hales Engineering, prepared a table showing a good part of the possible
businesses that might develop in Bluffdale, specifying the impact fee per square foot. All this will be
presented at the Council Meeting work session on October 28.

CERTIFICATION

IMPACT FEE FACILITY PLAN CERTIFICATION

Bluffdale City (City) and EFG Consulting (EFG) certify that the attached impact fee facilities plan:
    1. includes only the costs of public facilities that are:
             a. allowed under the Impact Fees Act; and
             b. actually incurred; or
             c. projected to be incurred or encumbered within six years after the day on which each
                 impact fee is paid;
    2. does not include:
             a. costs of operation and maintenance of public facilities;
             b. costs for qualifying public facilities that will raise the level of service for the facilities,
                 through impact fees, above the level of service that is supported by existing residents; or
             c. an expense for overhead, unless the expense is calculated pursuant to a methodology
                 that is consistent with generally accepted cost accounting practices and the
                 methodological standards set forth by the federal Office of Management and Budget for
                 federal grant reimbursement; and,
    3. complies in each and every relevant respect with the Impact Fees Act.

IMPACT FEE ANALYSIS CERTIFICATION

City and EFG certify that the attached impact fee analysis:
    1. includes only the costs of public facilities that are:
            a. allowed under the Impact Fees Act; and
            b. actually incurred; or
            c. projected to be incurred or encumbered within six years after the day on which each
                impact fee is paid;
    2. does not include:
            a. costs of operation and maintenance of public facilities;
            b. costs for qualifying public facilities that will raise the level of service for the facilities,
                through impact fees, above the level of service that is supported by existing residents;
            c. an expense for overhead, unless the expense is calculated pursuant to a methodology
                that is consistent with generally accepted cost accounting practices and the
                methodological standards set forth by the federal Office of Management and Budget for
                federal grant reimbursement;
    3. offsets costs with grants or other alternate sources of payment; and,
    4. complies in each and every relevant respect with the Impact Fees Act.




2020 IFFP & IFA – September Update                    1
CAVEATS

EFG joins in these certifications with the following caveats:
   1. All of the recommendations for implementations of the IFFP made in the IFFP documents or in
        the IFA documents are followed by the City staff and elected officials.
   2. If all or a substantial portion of the IFFP or IFA are modified or amended by the City, this
        certification is no longer valid.
   3. All information provided to our team is assumed to be correct, complete, and accurate. This
        includes information provided by the City as well as outside sources.


NOTE ON THIS SEPTEMBER UPDATE

This updated document amends the original approved in February 2020. The changes are highlighted in
this blue color throughout the document in the appropriate places but are summarized here.

Transportation – The City expects to receive $10m from interagency funds to pay for the project along
14600 South. The remaining portions of this project will pay for current deficiency or an increase in
level of service. Therefore, this project has been removed from the analysis. The application of this fee
has also been clarified to include PM entering trips by each land use type.

Water – The State of Utah determined that the City’s storage at the Metro Tank on the east side of the
system can be counted toward the City’s storage level of service requirements. This pushes off the
development of a new tank on the east side for another 10 to 15 years. This project has been removed
from the impact fee.

Public Safety – On recommendation from Chief James, the City will not require a ladder truck for the
next 6 years. A fire truck will be needed instead. The cost of the 2 ladder trucks have been updated to
reflect the likely cost of the purchases.




2020 IFFP & IFA – September Update                  2
2
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

The purpose of this IFFP and IFA is to fulfill the requirements of Utah Code Title 11 Chapter 36a (Impact
Fee Act) to enable the City to enact impact fees within the City for the following utilities: Parks &
Recreation, Public Safety, Storm Water, Transportation, and Water. The following is a summary of the
IFFP inputs. The IFFP and IFA will identify the maximum allowable impact fee. The City Council may
choose to adopt a fee lower than the maximum but not higher.

SERVICE AREA

The Service Area for each of the five impact fees is the entire City.

DEMAND ANALYSIS

The City estimated demand over the next ten years by identifying the areas which are most likely to
develop. Unit counts were assigned to each area based upon current land use. Units were then converted
to Equivalent Residential Units or Connections (ERU or ERC). Currently the City has 5,579 ERCs and is
projected to add 2,941 ERCs/ERUs in the next ten years. Of that number, 2,424 are projected to be
residential units and 517 are projected to be non-residential. In addition, this growth pattern is projected
to add 38,962 new daily traffic trips or 1,800 PM entering trips.

For more detailed methodology see the Section 3: Demand Analysis.

LEVEL OF SERVICE

The impact fee act (Act) requires that the City identify the current level of service (LOS) for each utility.
The following is a summary of the LOS for each utility. Greater detail can be found Sections 4-8.

    ▪    Parks & Recreation: Investment ($) / Residential ERU: $10,562
    ▪    Public Safety:
             o Facility (Residential and Commercial): Square Feet of Building Space / ERU: 4.61SF
             o Equipment (Commercial): Equipment ($) / Commercial ERU: $3,369
    ▪    Transportation: Service Level D and above
    ▪    Storm Water:
             o Storage: 100-year storm event (24-hour period) that fully drains in 48 hours
             o Conveyance: 10-year storm event (24-hour period)
    ▪    Water

There is currently no excess capacity in Parks & Recreation, Public Safety, Storm Water, and Water. Parks
& Recreation and Public Safety are both currently at the LOS. Storm Water and Water are below the
current LOS and will require additional projects to meet the LOS.

            FUNDING OF FUTURE FACILITIES

            The City has not included any plans to issue debt for any future facilities. This does not mean the City will
            not choose to do so. However, this analysis has assumed no interest carry cost as part of any impact fees.
            The City has been successful in receiving some grant funds for projects and will continue to seek these
            funds in the future. Should grant funds be received which will fund any of the projects in this report, the
            City will revisit the impact fee to ensure compliance with the Act. Currently, the City anticipates meeting
            the LOS for each system by funding new projects with cash. Impact fees are a critical component of
            generated that proportional cash to fund or repay future projects.

            MAXIMUM ALLOWABLE IMPACT FEES

            The following are the maximum allowable impact fees for each utility.

                 ▪    Parks & Recreation: $10,562/Residential ERU
                 ▪    Public Safety:
                          o $2,239/Residential ERU
                          o $4,571/Commercial ERU (one commercial unit)
                 ▪    Transportation: $6,738/PM Entering Trip
                          o See Appendix C for full land use table with fees
                 ▪    Storm Water: $494/ERU
                 ▪    Water: $4,485/ERU

DEMAND ANALYSIS

The demand over the next ten years was estimated by the City through a process of identifying the areas
which are most likely to develop. Figure A: Projected Growth Areas and Unit Counts identifies the parcels
that are likely to develop, the land use type for each, and the estimated number of new units to be
developed. Table 6: New Units provides a summary of the number of new units by land use type. Figure
B: Equivalent Residential Connections/Units converts unit counts into equivalent residential connections
or units and depicts the growth in those units over the next 10 and 30 years. This analysis determined
there would be 2,941 new ERC/ERUs (2,424 residential and 517 non-residential) and 38,962 new daily
trips.

PARKS & RECREATION IFFP & IFA

Bluffdale City provides park and recreation facilities to its citizens. This section will describe the following
items in accordance with the Act for Parks & Recreation.

    ▪    service area,
    ▪    demand within the service area,
    ▪    the current level of service,
    ▪    excess capacity in the current system to meet demand,
    ▪    future facilities required to meet demand,
    ▪    funding plans for future facilities,
    ▪    the maximum allowable impact fee new residential growth would pay to cover the proportionate
         share of excess capacity and new facilities, and
    ▪    the staff recommendation for impact fees to be charged.

SERVICE AREA & DEMAND

The service area for the Parks & Recreation impact fee is the entire City. The projected demand within
the service area is 2,424 new residential units or ERUs over the next 10 years. Parks & Recreation impact
fees are only charged to residential units.

CURRENT LEVEL OF SERVICE

The unit of measurement for the LOS is investment per residential unit or ERU. The LOS for Parks &
Recreation was calculated through an inventory of all current Parks & Recreation facilities including the
monetary value of these facilities. The value of these facilities is currently $43,092,524. Calculation details
can be found in Appendix A: Parks and Recreation Values. This total value was then divided by the
current number of residential ERUs in the City which is 4,080. The current LOS for Parks & Recreation is
$10,562 of investment per ERU.

EXCESS CAPACITY, FUTURE FACILITIES, & FUNDING PLAN

Because the LOS for Parks & Recreation uses the value of all current facilities, there is no excess capacity
in the system.

In order to maintain the LOS of $10,562/ERU with an additional 2,424 ERUs within the service area, the
City would be required to invest $25.6m in new facilities. The City will utilize taxes, fees, impact fees,
grants and may use bonds to construct future facilities. Although debt may be used, the City has not
assumed any interest expense in this calculation. As will be described in the Staff Recommendation
subsection, the City may choose to reduce the impact fee which will reduce the need for this level of
investment.



2020 IFFP & IFA – September Update                    13
MAXIMUM ALLOWABLE IMPACT FEE & STAFF RECOMMENDATION

The maximum allowable impact fee that can be charged to new residential growth is $10,562 per ERU.
Commercial ERUs will not be charged a Parks & Recreation impact fee. Impact fee credits can be received
for installing future facilities that meet the LOS.

City staff recognizes that the City has a large amount of Parks & Recreation facilities including Salt Lake
County facilities. For this reason, City staff recommends to the City Council a fee of $6,500 per ERU which
is $4,062 less than the maximum allowable.

PUBLIC SAFETY IFFP & IFA

Bluffdale City provides public safety services to the City including fire and police protection. This section
will describe the following items in accordance with the Act for Public Safety.

    ▪    service area,
    ▪    demand within the service area,

residential.

The Facility portion of the impact fee is charged to all ERUs. The Equipment portion is only charged to
commercial ERUs.

EXCESS CAPACITY, FUTURE FACILITIES, & FUNDING PLAN

Because the LOS for Public Safety uses the value of all current facilities, there is no excess capacity in the
system.

In order to maintain the facility LOS of 4.61 per ERU with an additional 2,941 ERUs within the service area,
the City would be required to build 13,559 square feet of new facilities. The City has estimated that it will
build this facility by 2029 which is estimated to cost $6.6m which is the cost of the 2019 station inflated
at 3% for construction inflation. In order to provide rolling stock for commercial, the City will buy two fire
trucks at $600k each in the next several years.

The City would utilize taxes, fees, impact fees, grants and may use bonds or equipment leases to construct
and/or acquire future facilities and/or equipment. Although debt may be used, the City has not assumed
any interest expense in this calculation.

MAXIMUM ALLOWABLE IMPACT FEE & STAFF RECOMMENDATION

The maximum allowable impact fee that can be charged to for future facilities is $2,239 per ERU. The
maximum allowable impact fee that can be charged to commercial ERUs for equipment is an additional
$2,332. Commercial ERUs would thus pay $4,571 per ERU.

City staff recommends charging the maximum allowable impact fee for public safety.

STORM WATER IFFP & IFA

The City of Bluffdale has designed and constructed a stormwater drainage system to reduce flooding and
minimize stormwater pollution into receiving waters. Flooding risk is generally mitigated by detaining,
retaining, or infiltrating stormwater before it reaches the receiving water. This helps ensure that public
and private properties have as minimal flooding risk as reasonably possible.

This section will describe the following items in accordance with the Act for Storm Water.

    ▪    service area,
    ▪    demand within the service area,
    ▪    the current level of service,
    ▪    excess capacity in the current system to meet demand,
    ▪    future facilities required to meet demand,
    ▪    funding plans for future facilities,
    ▪    the maximum allowable impact fee new growth would pay to cover the proportionate share of
         excess capacity and new facilities, and
    ▪    the staff recommendation for impact fees to be charged.

SERVICE AREA & DEMAND

The service area for the Storm Water impact fee is the entire City. The projected demand within the
service area is 2,941 new ERUs.

CURRENT LEVEL OF SERVICE

The City Storm Water Design Standards Manual defines the level of service for stormwater systems. All
stormwater storage must be sized to accommodate the 100 year (1% chance of annual occurrence) 24-
hour event. Stormwater conveyance systems must be sized to convey the 10-year (10% chance of annual
occurrence) 24-hour event. All open-air storage systems must drain fully within 48 hours to prevent the
growth of mosquitoes and other water borne pathogens. This plan assumes that all new development
provide stormwater conveyance/storage for their own property and the city will provide stormwater
services for all public rights-of-way. If a private development wishes to utilize a public facility, they may
be required to expand the receiving stormwater facility to provide adequate capacity.

The Equivalent Residential Unit (ERU) for the storm drain system is an estimate of the runoff contribution
that a single-family home sends into the storm drain system. The assumption is that each single-family
home has at least 2,700 square feet of impervious area.

To estimate the impact of Non-residential zones to the storm drain system, we must identify their amount
of impervious area. City ordinances require a minimum of 15% of the property be landscaped. Typically,
the remainder is constructed as impervious area (buildings, parking lots, sidewalks, etc.). To determine
the storm drain impact for a one acre, non-residential site, the amount of impervious area is assumed to

2020 IFFP & IFA – September Update                   17
        be 85% or 37,026 SF (43,560 SF x 85% = 37,026 SF). One ERU is 2,700 SF so the amount of ERU’s in a non-
        residential site is determined by: 37,026 SF/2,700 SF = 13.71 ERUs.

        EXCESS CAPACITY, FUTURE FACILITIES, & FUNDING PLAN

        Portions of the Storm Water system are currently deficient. There is thus no excess capacity in the system.

        In order to reach and maintain the LOS with an additional growth within the service area, the City will
        construct the facilities listed in Table 10. Storm Water New Projects. Of the $3.5m in new storm water
        facilities, $1.8m will serve new growth. These projects will serve 3,603 new growth ERUs.

                                                                                     New                          New

                                                                                    Growth                      Growth

13800 South Drainage                                      807,000          90%          10%         726,300        80,700
Bluffdale South System                                  1,644,000           3%          97%          47,676     1,596,324

Total                                                  $3,472,000                               $1,692,876    $1,779,124
                 Table 10. Storm Water New Projects

        The City will utilize taxes, fees, impact fees, grants and may use bonds to construct future facilities.
        Although debt may be used, the City has not assumed any interest expense in this calculation.

        MAXIMUM ALLOWABLE IMPACT FEE & STAFF RECOMMENDATION

        The maximum allowable impact fee that can be charged to new growth is calculated by dividing the total
        cost of the new growth projects ($1.8m) by the ERUs these facilities will serve (3,603). This calculates to
        $494 per ERU.

        City staff recommends that the City Council adopt the maximum allowable impact fee.

TRANSPORTATION IFFP & IFA

Bluffdale City owns transportation facilities within the City to provide service to its constituents. The State
and County also own transportation facilities. These non-city owned facilities are considered in meeting
the demands of the system. However, funding of these facilities are not included in this analysis. This
section will describe the following items in accordance with the Act for Transportation.

    ▪    service area,
    ▪    demand within the service area,
    ▪    the current level of service,
    ▪    excess capacity in the current system to meet demand,
    ▪    future facilities required to meet demand,
    ▪    funding plans for future facilities,
    ▪    the maximum allowable impact fee new growth would pay to cover the proportionate share of
         excess capacity and new facilities, and
    ▪    the staff recommendation for impact fees to be charged.

SERVICE AREA & DEMAND

The service area for the Transportation impact fee is the entire City. The projected demand within the
service area is 2,941 new ERUs which will generate 38,962 new daily trips by 2030 or 1,800 new PM
Entering trips.

CURRENT LEVEL OF SERVICE

The unit of measurement for the LOS service level D or higher. The service level is a measure of the
capacity of roads and intersections. The City determines transportation capacity by running
transportation modules that determine the number of trips by new growth type and the needed facilities
to meet that demand including city-owned and non-city-owned facilities.

these roads minus impact fee payments made to construct the roads is provided in Table 11.
Transportation Excess Capacity. These facilities will be utilized as part of the system to meet the future
demand.

        2020 IFFP & IFA                                     Appendix
ITE Land Use Glossary:

110, General Light Industrial - A light industrial facility is a free-standing facility devoted to a single use.
The facility has an emphasis on activities other than manufacturing and typically has minimal office space.

130, Industrial Park - An industrial park contains a number of industrial or related facilities. It is
characterized by a mix of manufacturing, service, and warehouse facilities with a wide variation in the
proportion of each type of use from one location to another.

140, Manufacturing - A manufacturing facility is an area where the primary activity is the conversion of
raw materials or parts into finished products. In addition, manufacturing facilities generally also have
office, warehouse, research, and associated functions.

150, Warehousing - A warehouse is primarily devoted to the storage of materials, but it may also include
office and maintenance areas.

151, Mini-Warehouse (Self-Storage) - A mini-warehouse is a building in which a number of storage units
or vaults are rented for the storage of goods. They are typically referred to as “self-storage” facilities.

210, Single-Family Detached Housing - Single-family detached housing includes all single-family detached
homes on individual lots. A typical site surveyed is a suburban subdivision.

220, Multifamily Housing (Low-Rise) - Low-rise multifamily housing includes apartments, townhouses,
and condominiums located within the same building with at least three other dwelling units and that have
one or two levels (floors).

221, Multifamily Housing (Mid-Rise) - Mid-rise multifamily housing includes apartments, townhouses,
and condominiums located within the same building with at least three other dwelling units and that have
between three and 10 levels (floors).

251, Senior Adult Housing—Detached - Senior adult housing consists of detached independent living
developments, including retirement communities, age-restricted housing, and active adult communities.

252, Senior Adult Housing—Attached - Senior adult housing consists of attached independent living
developments, including retirement communities, age-restricted housing, and active adult communities.

254, Assisted Living - An assisted living complex is a residential setting that provides either routine general
protective oversight or assistance with activities necessary for independent living to mentally or physically
limited persons. Its services typically include dining, housekeeping, social and physical activities,
medication administration, and transportation.

310, Hotel - A hotel is a place of lodging that provides sleeping accommodations and supporting facilities
such as restaurants, cocktail lounges, meeting and banquet rooms or convention facilities, limited
recreational facilities (pool, fitness room), and/or other retail and service shops.

520, Elementary School - Both public and private schools are included in this land use.

522, Middle School/Junior High School - Both public and private schools are included in this land use.

530, High School - Both public and private schools are included in this land use.

2020 IFFP & IFA                                    Appendix
534, Private School (K-8) - A private school (K-8) primarily serves students attending kindergarten through
the eighth grade but may also include students beginning with pre–K classes.

536, Private School (K-12) - A private school (K-12) primarily serves students attending kindergarten
through the 12th grade but may also include those beginning with pre–K classes.

560, Church - A church is a building in which public worship services are held. A church houses an assembly
hall or sanctuary; it may also house meeting rooms, classrooms, and, occasionally, dining, catering, or
party facilities.

565, Day Care Center - A day care center is a facility where care for pre-school age children is provided,
normally during the daytime hours. Day care facilities generally include classrooms, offices, eating areas
and playgrounds.

620, Nursing Home - A nursing home is any facility whose primary function is to provide care for persons
who are unable to care for themselves. Skilled nurses and nursing aides are present 24 hours a day at
these sites. Nursing homes are occupied by residents who do little or no driving; traffic is primarily
generated by employees, visitors, and deliveries.

630, Clinic - A clinic is any facility that provides limited diagnostic and outpatient care but is unable to
provide prolonged in-house medical and surgical care. Clinics commonly have lab facilities, supporting
pharmacies, and a wide range of services (compared to the medical office, which may only have
specialized or individual physicians).

710, General Office Building - A general office building houses multiple tenants; it is a location where
affairs of businesses, commercial or industrial organizations, or professional persons or firms are
conducted.

720, Medical-Dental Office Building - A medical-dental office building is a facility that provides diagnoses
and outpatient care on a routine basis but is unable to provide prolonged in-house medical and surgical
care.

770, Business Park (Flex Space) - A business park consists of a group of flex-type or incubator one- or two-
story buildings served by a common roadway system. The tenant space is flexible and lends itself to a
variety of uses; the rear side of the building is usually served by a garage door. Tenants may be start-up
companies or small mature companies that require a variety of space. The space may include offices, retail
and wholesale stores, restaurants, recreational areas and warehousing, manufacturing, light industrial, or
scientific research functions. The average mix is 20 to 30 percent office/commercial and 70 to 80 percent
industrial/warehousing.

820, Shopping Center (General Retail) - A shopping center is an integrated group of commercial
establishments that is planned, developed, owned, and managed as a unit. This land use represents
general retail.

931, Quality Restaurant - This land use consists of high quality, full-service eating establishments with a
typical duration of stay of at least one hour. This type of restaurant often requests and sometimes requires
reservations and is generally not part of a chain. Patrons commonly wait to be seated, are served by a
waiter/ waitress, order from menus and pay for meals after they eat.


2020 IFFP & IFA                                   Appendix
932, High-Turnover (Sit-Down) Restaurant - This land use consists of sit-down, full-service eating
establishments with typical duration of stay of approximately one hour. This type of restaurant is usually
moderately priced and frequently belongs to a restaurant chain. Patrons commonly wait to be seated, are
served by a waiter/waitress, order from menus and pay for their meal after they eat.

934, Fast-Food Restaurant with Drive-Through Window - This category includes fast-food restaurants
with drive-through windows. This type of restaurant is characterized by a large drive-through clientele,
long hours of service and high turnover rates for eat-in customers.

945, Gasoline/Service Station with Convenience Market - This land use includes gasoline/service stations
with convenience markets where the primary business is the fueling of motor vehicles. These service
stations may also have ancillary facilities for servicing and repairing motor vehicles and may have a car
wash. The gross floor area of the convenience market is between 2,000 and 3,000 gross square feet and
the number of vehicle fueling positions is at least 10.

960, Super Convenience Market/Gas Station - This land use includes gasoline/service stations with
convenience markets where there is significant business related to the sale of convenience items and the
fueling of motor vehicles. The gross floor area of the convenience market is at least 3,000 gross square
feet and the number of vehicle fueling positions is at least 10.

31            2.1    Discussion Relating to the Redevelopment of the Draper Prison Site, Presenter,
32                   Alan Matheson, Executive Director of the Point of the Mountain State Land
33                   Authority.
34
35   Mayor Timothy introduced Alan Matheson, Executive Director for the Point of the Mountain State
36   Land Authority, who reported that the Prison site consists of 700 acres and has been discussed for
37   several years. In January, site selectors from outside the State of Utah visited the property and
38   stated that there is no other opportunity like it in the country. Mr. Matheson stressed the
39   importance of the project.
40



                                                     1
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                     BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                        MEETING MINUTES
                                     Wednesday, September 9, 2020

 1   Mr. Matheson reported that when the site was first discussed, The Point of the Mountain
 2   Development Commission staged a two-year process to listen to public feedback and preferences.
 3   The priorities identified are included in the statute that created The Point of the Mountain State
 4   Land Authority. The Council viewed the “What’s the Point” video.
 5
 6   The statute for the project includes the following directives:
 7
 8              •   Create jobs and workforce development;
 9              •   Create quality of life on the site and in surrounding communities;
10              •   Coordinate closely with surrounding communities;
11              •   Provide open space;
12              •   Offer world-class transit;
13              •   Provide recreational opportunities; and
14              •   Explore nationally recognized research institutions.
15
16   The ultimate goal for the site was to make life better for the people in the State of Utah. Mr.
17   Matheson explained that the site has the potential to generate economic activity, which will provide
18   cutting-edge jobs for young people and keep them in Utah. If done correctly, a sustainable
19   community can be created that sets a model for low emissions, low energy use, and low water use
20   that other developers can follow to improve the environment within the State.
21
22   Mr. Matheson highlighted the following guiding principles established by the organization:
23
24              •   Act in the public interest;
25              •   Think regionally;
26              •   Take the long view to sustain value;
27              •   Set the standard in the State;
28              •   Be open and transparent; and
29              •   Act with integrity.
30

                                          MEETING MINUTES
                                       Wednesday, September 9, 2020

 1   Mayor Timothy thanked Mr. Matheson for his expertise and commitment to the public.
 2
 3           2.2       Discussion Relating to Proposed Text Amendment – Home Occupation
 4                     Business License, Staff Presenter, Todd Sheeran.
 5
 6   City Attorney, Todd Sheeran, presented the staff report and stated that the reason the Home
 7   Occupation portion of the Notice of Pending Ordinance was last was due to the significant effort
 8   that will be required by the Council. Mr. Sheeran reviewed the current portion of the ordinance
 9   and stated that the regulations that have posed problems are listed in Chapter 11 of the ordinance.
10   Specific examples were given. Major guidelines were not given for what to consider for major
11   Home Occupations. The only prohibition in the ordinance was for medical uses.
12
13   Mr. Sheeran stated that the purpose of Home Occupations is to provide peace, quiet, and domestic
14   tranquility within all residential zones and allow flexibility for residents to operate a business out
15   of their home. The impacts the City desires to regulate consist of traffic, parking, and noise, which
16   are the most common complaints about a business operated out of a home.
17
18   Mr. Sheeran reported that he, as well as Finance Director, Bruce Kartchner and City
19   Planner/Economic Development Director, Grant Crowell, have led the project. They identified
20   the following six categories for home occupations:
21
22                 •   Residents who do business in their home with the occasional client;
23                 •   Residents that store items at their home;
24                 •   Residents who have employees or clients picking up and dropping off items;
25                 •   Residents that have employees working at their home;
26                 •   Residents that teach children in their home; and
27                 •   Residents that operate a seasonal business from their home.
28
29   Mr. Crowell stated that the Council needs to determine what should be regulated and allowed in
30   the ordinance. If it is not detailed, it will be difficult to enforce.
31



                                                         4
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                      BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                         MEETING MINUTES
                                      Wednesday, September 9, 2020

1    Mr. Sheeran confirmed with City Engineer, Michael Fazio, that the average home has a traffic
 2   count of 10 trips per day. He asked the Council what the traffic count should be for the above
 3   item. Traci Crockett was in favor of a regulation for traffic counts and agreed that determining the
 4   number of trips per business is difficult.
 5
 6   Dave Kallas stated was unaware of what the traffic count should be, however, he would be more
 7   sensitive if he was a neighbor who operates a business from his home. He did, however, favor a
 8   seasonal business operation option.
 9
10   Mr. Kartchner reported that staff has received a proposal from citizens to calculate trips based on
11   the average number of trips throughout the year for seasonal businesses. Mayor Timothy stated
12   that there will be an impact on a seasonal business and the success of the business should not be
13   at the expense of the neighbors.
14
15   Wendy Aston suggested that the Council consider the level of traffic, noise, and dust and determine
16   if a use is appropriate for a neighborhood. Mark Hales agreed and stated that referring to noise
17   and impact, a business should not be assigned to be a home occupation and the same status should
18   remain.
19
20   Mr. Sheeran asked how parking should be addressed for the above item.               Traci Crockett
21   recommended that it be similar to the parking requirement for basement apartments. Wendy Aston
22   agreed with Mayor Timothy and stated that there is a delicate balance between successful
23   businesses and its neighbors. Mr. Sheeran responded that drafting the Code is challenging and
24   Riverton City’s Home Occupation section strives to balance this. Drafting a Use Code is an option.
25   Wendy Aston remarked that this may not be the right approach and agreed with Council Member
26   Crockett’s approach about parking.
27

29   discussion, Mr. Sheeran asked how storage should be regulated if it is uncovered. Mayor Timothy
30   stated that residents have complained about loud large vehicles in the early morning hours at home

17   4.     Presentation and Discussion Relating to the Proposed Amendments to the Impact Fee
18          Facilities Plan and Impact Fee Analysis for Parks and Recreation, Storm Drainage,
19          Water Public Safety, and Transportation, Staff Presenter, Michael Fazio.
20
21   Mr. Fazio presented the staff report and stated that Mr. Kartchner has also been involved in the
22   project. Staff has reviewed the impact fees due to complaints about them being excessive.
23   Transportation, water, and fire were closely considered, and it was been determined that the impact
24   fees can be further reduced. Due to funding that will be received from outside agencies for the
25   14600 South Project, the funding required to collect was decreased by $10 million, which will
26   reduce the transportation impact fee.
27
28   Mr. Kartchner reported that the City is not capable of pursuing an $18 million project and the only
29   way to obtain the funds is to receive a grant or participation from another agency. The City has
30   been successful over the last 12 years in receiving these types of funds, so staff was optimistic.
31   Mr. Kartchner was not confident that the overall project will cost $18 million based on the cost of




                                                      11
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                        MEETING MINUTES

 1   construction, particularly the cost of the trestle. Mayor Timothy did not include the trestle in the
 2   cost and was confident that the City will receive funding for it.
 3
 4   Mr. Fazio reported that Alan Matheson, Executive Director of the Point of the Mountain State
 5   Land Authority, is confident that the State will invest a significant amount of money in the area.
 6   Mayor Timothy was confident that the City will receive the needed funds to improve the road and
 7   widen the ridge on 14600 South. Mr. Fazio reported that due to this potential funding, staff reduced
 8   the Transportation Impact Fee by $30 per daily trip.
 9
10   Staff also realized there is a storage area being leased at the Metro Tank that the City was not
11   allowed to use; however, the State has communicated that it can now be included in the City’s
12   Master Plan. With this, the City decided to postpone the construction of a two-million-gallon
13   storage tank on the east side by Geneva Rock out 15 years. This will not impact current projects
14   and reduces the Equivalent Residential Unit (“ERU”) cost substantially for the Water Impact Fee.
15
16   The City previously planned to purchase a ladder truck at an estimated cost of $1.2 million.
17   Mr. Kartchner reported that the City’s Interlocal Agreements will cover the ladder truck. The
18   City’s more immediate needs for the next six years are for two new regular trucks. This is a
19   conversation that staff will be approaching the Council about and to potentially order one of the
20   trucks sooner rather than later. Due to the lead time needed for the trucks, a budget adjustment
21   may be needed.

23   The City’s impact fee comparisons with other cities were displayed. The Parks and Transportation
24   fees were still high but comparable to what other cities charge. The Stormwater Fees were lower
25   as compared to other cities. Mr. Kartchner reported that when the impact fee schedule was passed
26   in February, the Council decided to reduce the fee by 30%.           This current change did not
27   contemplate a reduction. At that time, the Council desired to promote commercial development
28   and the Transportation Impact Fee does have an impact on commercial coming in. Staff had not
29   included a reduction. Mr. Kartchner asked the Council if the desire was to encourage commercial
30   growth.

 4   6.     CONTINUED FROM AUGUST 26, 2020 – Consideration and Vote on Ordinance
 5          2020-18, a City-Initiated Text Amendment Application to Amend Titles 11-2 and 11-
 6          22 of the Bluffdale City Land Use Ordinances, Revising the City’s Sign Code, Along
 7          with Associated Definitions Related Administrative Provisions, City of Bluffdale,
 8          Applicant, Staff Presenter, Grant Crowell.
 9
10   City Planner/Economic Development Director, Grant Crowell, presented the staff report and stated
11   that the above item has been in a process since March 11, 2020, when a Temporary Zoning
12   Ordinance was established limiting sign permits in the community while the City updated its Sign
13   Code. It had been determined that the Sign Code needs to be repealed and replaced. There is an
14   entirely new sign code, except for the provision for billboards, which was adopted in 2015. One
15   of the reasons the rezoning was established was because the default for signs in the community
16   were pole signs. There are current zones within the City that include signage requirements.
17
18   The most significant discussion involved Electronic Message Centers (“EMC”). The Planning
19   Commission recommended that if a business is allowed a sign, the face of the sign could be used
20   as an EMC without limitations. Examples were given of where EMC are located within the
21   community. The above matter addresses them more specifically.
22
23   Mr. Crowell was asked to address specific unique businesses. A commercial veterinary hospital
24   in the R-143 zone near Bangerter Highway desired a pole sign on Bangerter Highway, however,
25   the Planning Commission did not approve the pole sign or the EMC. Mr. Crowell contacted the
26   sign representative and they were aware of the denial; however, it has been an issue as the Code
27   has been crafted.
28
29   Staff desired to apply EMCs to locations near the freeway and large development multi-tenant
30   buildings. Staff improved the administration, the application requirements, and the clarity of the
31   signage requirements.
32



                                                    16
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                     BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                        MEETING MINUTES
                                     Wednesday, September 9, 2020

 1   Mr. Crowell reported that school signs are regulated by State law, however, it is not necessary.
 2   Summit Academy has applied for a permit, however, other schools have not. The Council needs
 3   to determine what to require from schools. Churches are also regulated; however, they can also
 4   be exempt if desired by the Council. The ordinance addressing municipal uses was read. The
 5   Planning Commission asked why the City is allowed an eight-foot LED sign, but a commercial
 6   developer is not. It was noted that the City needs to communicate, and staff has determined that
 7   the size is appropriate. Information on banners in the ordinance was reviewed. The above item
 8   does not include LED billboards, however, information on the hotel billboard was discussed.
 9
10   Mr. Crowell stated that with regard to LED brightness there are three different brightness
11   measurements. One uses 0.3 foot-candles over ambient light, which is the same as the billboard
12   standard. A more accurate way to measure LED brightness is luminance versus illuminance.
13   Luminance is defined as luminous intensity projected on a given area and direction. Illuminance
14   refers to the amount of light falling onto a given surface area. This is a less extensive meter and
15   provides an approximation of what actually emanates out of the EMC. Another way to measure
16   screen brightness is to use a candela or a nit. The meters for this type of reading cost $2,000 and
17   training and certification are required to use them.
18
19   If the Council desires to be more detailed, staff has resources from the International Dark-Sky
20   Association to assist in measuring brightness. Mr. Crowell hoped to reach an agreement that
21   addresses the basic needs of the community before the Temporary Zoning Ordinance expires on
22   Friday, September 11, 2020.
23
24   Dave Kallas thanked Mr. Crowell for his efforts and reported that the website referenced by
25   Mr. Crowell was created to provide recommendations for LED signs. He was in favor of moving
26   forward and return to revise the LED portion of the ordinance. It was recommended that a curfew
27   be required. Mr. Crowell reviewed examples of how curfews can be applied.
28
29   Wendy Aston appreciated the work done by staff and indicated that she originally pushed for the
30   item to be revised. She felt it was important for it to be passed tonight but also necessary to be



                                                      17
                            DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                     BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                        MEETING MINUTES
                                     Wednesday, September 9, 2020

 1   more detailed with the EMC. Mr. Crowell did not want the Council to feel pressure to take action.
 2   He explained that businesses with pending applications were waiting for the ordinance to be
 3   passed. Mr. Crowell reported that no businesses have applied for an EMC.
 4
 5   Traci Crockett was not in favor of taller pole signs and felt that the EMCs were of concern. She
 6   was willing to pass the matter as-is, with the intention being to revise the EMC section.
 7   Mr. Crowell stated that all references to the EMC could be removed until the section is revised.
 8   Mayor Timothy suggested that the Code specify that no EMC are allowed.
 9
10   Jeff Gaston asked if the revision would impact the Point of the Mountain Storages sign near I-15.
11   Mr. Crowell stated that the tall sign with the time and temperature was approved under the previous
12   provision, however, this is now a content-based regulation and should be avoided. The other EMC
13   is up, however, he was unsure if it meets all of the required criteria. Jeff Gaston stated that the
14   company is a positive community partner and should be exempt from the new ordinance because
15   the signs are already in. Dave Kallas agreed that any sign that is already up should remain.

32

34

36
37   3.       Form-Based Code and Design Standards Discussion.
38
39   City Planner/Economic Development Director, Grant Crowell provided an overview and
40   description of a Form-Based Code and described how it might be implemented. He compared




                                                   1
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                 BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                           BLUFFDALE CITY PLANNING COMMISSION
                              SPECIAL JOINT MEETING MINUTES
                                 Wednesday, September 9, 2020

 1   form-based codes to traditional zoning approaches to determine the degree to which they might be
 2   both applicable and desirable.
 3
 4   Mr. Crowell explained that form-based code creates new law and is a legislative process by the
 5   City Council. Once adopted, it would become an administrative process. The design standards
 6   would be administered by staff or the Planning Commission. Form-Based Code was defined as a
 7   land development regulation that fosters predictable results by using physical form (rather than
 8   separation of uses) as the organizing principle. Form-based code is a regulation, and not just a
 9   guideline. It is adopted into city, town, or county law. Form-based code offers a powerful
10   alternative to conventional zoning regulation. There are pros and cons to the various approaches
11   that are implemented to regulate land use and zoning.
12
13   Mr. Crowell reported that zoning and accompanying regulations can be very onerous. The City’s
14   role needs to be determined by land development. Mayor Timothy commented that one of the
15   goals is to be more friendly in terms of fostering economic and business development. The initial
16   impetus for looking at form-based code was to identify potential options to facilitate economic
17   development that address all of the complexities of the City’s needs and vision.
18
19   In response to a question regarding how form-based code has less control over the use and more
20   control over the design, Mr. Crowell explained that the key term is “placemaking,” which relates
21   to the physical form. Form-based code is a theoretical construct for regulating land use. There are
22   templates to inform the process but they do not have universal applicability.         Mr. Crowell
23   explained that in conversations with other planners, one point that has been emphasized is the
24   determination of what constitutes the City’s center and where placemaking matters the most.
25
26   Mr. Crowell explained that “placemaking” requires a clear vision and purpose, including streets,
27   the public realm, buildings, open space, parks, landscaping, parking, signs, and land uses. He
28   noted that the City has implemented bike lanes well but not a lot of landscaping in City projects
29   due to cost. A form-based code would address the types of street designs used for different parts
30   of the community.     Walkability, parking limitations, and locations were determined to be


                                                     2
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                 BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                           BLUFFDALE CITY PLANNING COMMISSION
                              SPECIAL JOINT MEETING MINUTES
                                 Wednesday, September 9, 2020

1    significant considerations. It was noted that parking is typically in front of buildings so drivers
2    can see their parking options.
3
4    Mr. Crowell next addressed building type. There was a lot of emphasis on mixed-use, which
5    includes housing. Many form-based codes do not address density. Instead, they focus on building
6    types, architecture, and parking. It was noted that the specificity of building materials and facades
7    is difficult to regulate. For example, Bluffdale does not have a general theme due to the diversity
8    of building designs that exist. Color schemes, roof designs, and siding come in and out of style.
9    A form-based code would dictate the types of buildings that are allowed in different parts of the
10   City. Mr. Crowell explained that if the City Council opted to implement form-based codes, it
11   would be necessary to include diagrams to clarify the expectations.
12
13   Mr. Crowell reported that setback requirements would be impacted by form-based codes. He
14   added that architecture could be addressed more thoroughly in multi-family settings. Landscaping,
15   open space, and pocket parks would need to be addressed in a form-based code.                 Water
16   conservation can also be included. Mr. Crowell explained that form-based codes, in many
17   instances, are based on formulas the City would not want to implement in the suburbs because they
18   emphasize shared parking and less parking. The City Council would have the authority to
19   determine what uses would and would not be allowed in a zone.
20
21   With regard to traditional zoning, Mr. Crowell explained that it works in one area of the community
22   or zone, but not necessarily in another. In addition, it clearly identifies what the vision is for a
23   particular area of the City and instead, relies on the Table of Allowed Uses. He pointed out that
24   mixing uses is difficult with traditional zoning.
25
26   Mr. Crowell explained that the challenge with form-based codes is that they are very rigorous to
27   create and require the creation of different districts. The process would be iterative and require
28   training and a commitment to adhere to the placemaking principles that are established when there
29   is pushback. City residents could also be asked to accept more permitted uses. The creation of a



                                                         3
                             DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                   BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                             BLUFFDALE CITY PLANNING COMMISSION
                                SPECIAL JOINT MEETING MINUTES
                                   Wednesday, September 9, 2020

 1   form-based code would require a comprehensive examination of the City’s construction standards
 2   and capital projects.
 3
 4   Mr. Crowell reported that the form-based code would not necessarily replace the existing City
 5   Code because of the creation of individual districts. A Building Design Code may be easier to
 6   develop, which could incorporate form-based code principles. It would also be possible to remove
 7   certain land uses to eliminate certain types of buildings.
 8
 9   Mr. Crowell opined that the General Plan update would provide an opportunity to consider new
10   zones and land uses to vet with the public. Plans to initiate the General Plan update were underway.
11   Once the document is completed, developers will be provided with a copy, which will simplify the
12   process since the parameters for architecture would be clearly spelled out.
13
14   Since true form-based codes are for town and urban centers, it may be a challenge to apply the
15   concept in Bluffdale. If the issue involves only building design, the City could address it without
16   a form-based code. Creating a City-wide form-based code would require a great deal of time and
17   expense. It would also restrict the creative options available to architects.
18
19   Design code updates may be an alternative approach to creating a new form-based code. They
20   would allow the City to consider a wide variety of issues, such as relief, color, circulation,
21   orientation, parking, display, outside storage, buffering, and screening. The City currently has a
22   Design Code that could serve as the basis for modifying the Design Code.
23
24   Planning Commission Chair Cragun asked how cities create form-based codes when they are
25   already built out. Mr. Crowell explained that some of the areas would be legal non-conforming.
26   Decisions could be made regarding what would be necessary to come into compliance and there
27   could be incentives or disincentives. In addition, some vacant properties have vesting attached to
28   them. For example, the GC-1 Land Use Table is very broad in terms of what is permitted and a
29   lot of land has been rezoned GC-1. The 2700 West Corridor is probably the most imminent




                                                       4
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                 BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                           BLUFFDALE CITY PLANNING COMMISSION
                              SPECIAL JOINT MEETING MINUTES
                                 Wednesday, September 9, 2020

 1   development taking place currently. If pending business opportunities are a threat, the City
 2   Council needs to act quickly and definitively.
 3
 4   Council Member Kallas suggested that a list of ideas or concerns be considered. He sought the
 5   consensus of the other City Council Members regarding whether a form-based code was right for
 6   Bluffdale. He observed that Bluffdale does not have a historic Main Street because it is more of a
 7   residential community that is encouraging new development. 2700 West would be a place where
 8   a design code could possibly accomplish what the City Council would like to see happen. One
 9   opportunity that is unique for Bluffdale is 14600 South from the freeway to the railroad. On 2700
10   West, there are one or two blocks where development is already planned. Council Member Kallas
11   reiterated the potential development value of 14600 South, particularly as the prison site is vacated.
12
13   Council Member Aston originally thought that the small section on 2700 West might be a good
14   pilot area to implement form-based code. She acknowledged, however, that it might be too late
15   for that. She commented that the design standards for 2700 West could accomplish what the City
16   would like to see happen. Council Member Crockett agreed that a form-based code might be too
17   late for 2700 West. The design standards would, therefore, be the best pathway for the area. She
18   was not sure the City was ready to seriously consider a transition to a form-based code and
19   acknowledged that it would be a lot of work to develop.
20
21   Council Member Hales believed that changing to a form-based code would be a major undertaking.
22   Mayor Timothy commented that even if a small area was selected to institute form-based code, it
23   would be a major commitment to develop. He believed that the issue was worthy of discussion
24   and consideration by the City Council. His takeaway from the discussion was that more emphasis
25   should be placed on the design standards. He believed that the consensus of the Council was to
26   hold off on developing a form-based code and consider something more long-term.
27
28   Mayor Timothy suggested that the City retain a consultant and encouraged staff to focus on short-
29   term issues. Mr. Crowell stated that retaining a consultant for a project of this scope would cost

 1   around $100,000. He pointed out that the City is already committing funds to update the General
 2   Plan.

 5   Standards. They apply City-wide; however, additional geographical areas could have specific
6    design standards applied to them. He noted that a Development Agreement is a quasi-form-based-
 7   code. He pointed out that window requirements are of particular concern. Mayor Timothy noted

 9   alternatives to a grocery store. One of the issues that was brought up was the power lines in the
10   Independence area, which will deter many from wanting to develop there. He believed that in the
11   future, the City should consider requiring all power lines be buried. He commented that the power
12   lines are ruining the look of the areas being developed.
13
14   City Attorney, Todd Sheeran reported that Rocky Mountain Power is guided by the Public Utilities
15   Section of the State Code - Title 54 that gives specific allowances to Rocky Mountain Power. With
16   new development, the City can negotiate an agreement with Rocky Mountain Power. If there is
17   an easement, Rocky Mountain Power can place the power line above ground. Mayor Timothy
18   stated that Rocky Mountain Power has indicated that they will bury the line but the City would
19   have to cover the cost. Mr. Sheeran stated that Title 54 specifies that the City has to pay the cost
20   difference. It was noted that burying a power line is much more expensive than placing it above
21   ground, so cities typically do not choose to do it. He offered to look into how other municipalities
22   have covered the cost.
23
24   Mr. Sheeran agreed to look into the viability of having a Special Service Area. Council Member
25   Gaston stated that it might be possible to get financial assistance from the State to bury the power
26   lines along 14600 South when the prison is vacated and the property is developed.

29   was confident that if one comes, others will follow.

37            a.     McDougal/Olsen SD Zone Proposal, Presenter, Steve McCutchan.

39   Steve McCutchan reported that they made a presentation 18 months ago and they fulfilled the

41   presence tonight was at the request of City Planner/Economic Development Director, Grant


                                                   1
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                    BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                       MEETING MINUTES
                                    Wednesday, September 23, 2020

 1   Crowell. Mr. McCutchan explained that the proposal is to have the property rezoned SD to build
 2   a mixed-use community to include commercial, possible flex space industrial, and residential. He
 3   explained that the property is located immediately north of Bringhurst Station. (southeast corner
 4   of Redwood Road and Porter Rockwell Boulevard). The application will not include the 10-acre
 5   property that McDougal/Olsen owns north of Porter Rockwell Boulevard as it is already zoned
 6   Commercial. The remaining property is approximately 50 acres.
 7
 8   Mr. McCutchan reported that the Utah Department of Transportation (“UDOT”) owns two parcels
 9   on the property, which are for the construction of Redwood Road and the associated drainage. An
10   application cannot be made on the center property until it is acquired from UDOT. Building around
11   the UDOT property would result in a less valuable commercial parcel. The proposal is to rezone
12   and develop as much of the area as possible. When there is a resolution with the UDOT property,
13   McDougal/Olsen will build on the remaining areas as well.
14
15   Mr. Crowell explained that Mr. McCutchan was at the Work Session because the previous SD zone
16   application had been discussed with the previous City Council. The landowners felt it would be
17   beneficial to have a conversation with the current City Council. Mr. Crowell noted that Mr.
18   McCutchan had already met the statutory requirement to meet with the City Council before
19   submitting an application and had paid the $3,000 application fee. Mr. Crowell believed it would
20   be beneficial to obtain feedback from the Council to better guide the application process.
21
22   Mr. McCutchan reported that the ultimate goal was to reach an agreement where the City and client
23   goals for the property are aligned. He believed the current proposal could be discussed over a
24   series of meetings. Mr. McCutchan shared the conceptual site plan with the Council. He noted
25   that one version of the site plan showed the development of the property with a small grocery store
26   and an office building. A trail would also be developed.

29   development. Mark Hales asked what the balance would be between commercial and flex space

                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

20          b.      Discussion of Eastern Bluffdale Economic Development Area Revitalization
21                  Project om 855 West 14730 South, Staff Presenter, Grant Crowell.
22
23   Mr. Crowell reported that along 14600 South, on the northern portion of Eastern Bluffdale, is the
24   Eastern Bluffdale Economic Development Area (“EDA”). An Economic Development Area is an
25   area that has been targeted for assessed value growth and job creation. The Redevelopment
26   Agency (“RDA”) Board of Directors will have an active role in the future of 14600 South. Growth
27   of taxable value is used to create money that is available for incentives, infrastructure development,
28   and facilitation. Mr. Crowell noted that legal counsel was in place from Smith Hartvigsen PLLC,
29   which included J. Craig Smith, Jay Springer, and Adam Long. The RDA determined it was
30   important to have an EDA and to focus it on more than just retail. Mr. Crowell reported that the




                                                       3
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                     BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                        MEETING MINUTES
                                     Wednesday, September 23, 2020

 1   EDA will focus on assessed growth, street improvements, and anything that would benefit the area
 2   as a whole.
 3
 4   Mr. Crowell presented slides of Eastern Bluffdale, including a gravel road and historical
 5   development areas in the community. He noted that there has been reinvestment in the area in the
 6   last five years. Mr. Crowell reported that one area used to be a stockpiling and dirt sifting yard for
 7   a landscaping company. It is now a community contributor with an open storage yard and a new
 8   building. Mr. Crowell discussed new buildings that had been completed and a rural gravel road.
 9   The gravel road did not have a lot of modern street improvements or utilities but there had been
10   development interest. Mr. Crowell noted that in recent years, a new building had been constructed
11   on the property at BD Bush Excavation.
12
13   In 2018, the City Council asked the following questions:
14
15      •   Should we work towards having a public street system in the Eastern Bluffdale area?
16      •   Will that help with the future growth of the area?
17
18   At the time, the City Council believed a public street system was appropriate and entered into a
19   Delay Agreement. The Delay Agreement was for six years, to determine whether or not there
20   would be a public road in that area. As a result, there are existing obligations to BD Bush
21   Excavation and a right-of-way dedication for a portion of the road. Mr. Crowell noted that there
22   was an existing use easement of the gravel facility for approximately 50 feet. It was deemed that
23   35 feet was the responsibility of BD Bush Excavation.
24
25   Mr. Crowell shared the BD Bush Excavation site plan. Staff had already shown the site plan
26   drawing to Brixton Partners and notified them that if the road went a certain way, it would go into
27   their proposed building. Mr. Crowell asked the City Council if the goal was still to have a public
28   road go through 856 West 14730 South. He wondered if it would increase the assessed value,
29   increase property values, maintain character, and fit into the vision of the 14600 South Project.
30   Mr. Crowell discussed ownership of various parcels of land and noted that there could be a
31   modification of the agreements between BD Bush Excavation and the City.

 2   Mayor Timothy asked about the number of Delay Agreements in place. Mr. Crowell stated that
 3   there was a Delay Agreement with BD Bush Excavation. No other property owners had Delay
 4   Agreements at this time. Mayor Timothy asked if Mr. Crowell wanted the City to build the road
 5   and then let the Delay Agreement and future impact fees pay for the project. Mr. Crowell clarified
 6   that he was trying to determine whether it was still a good idea for the community to follow up
 7   with the road development. Finance Director, Bruce Kartchner reported that the road is not
 8   currently on the Impact Fee Facilities Plan (“IFFP”) so it was not part of the Impact Fee Analysis
 9   (“IFA”). Staff would not be able to spend funds on the road unless the Council wanted it added.
10
11   Mr. Crowell noted that if the RDA agreed to some of the development, it would not necessarily
12   need to be part of the impact fee plan. Instead, the City could work with landowners to create a
13   phased approach. City Manager, Mark Reid clarified that impact fee funds could not be used but
14   EDA funds could be. Mayor Timothy asked if staff wanted to spend RDA money to build the road
15   as a way to incentivize development. Developers would then pay for their portion of the road
16   during development to pay back the RDA. Mr. Crowell believed there were some potential
17   partners. For reimbursement, staff would determine what the development share would be. Mr.
18   Crowell felt that capital from the EDA funds would need to be used if the City wanted certain
19   sections of the road paved in the near future.

22   Mr. Reid suggested possible ideas. Mr. Crowell noted that some EDA money was being reserved
23   for future development projects, related to the frontage road and tall hotels. He stated that what
24   was being discussed with the road was a Reimbursement Agreement or a modification of a
25   previous Delay Agreement, both of which would have to be approved.
26
27   Jeff Gaston asked what the cost would be to pave the road. City staff was unsure of the total cost
28   but encouraged the developers to speak to BD Bush Excavation. The suggested proposal was for
29   the developer to come up with a way to pave the road. Reimbursement would be given for a section
30   of the road.

40   Sheryl Babcock gave her address as 1626 West Desert Sage Circle and was present to discuss a
41   property located at 15149 South Buckskin Point Circle. Ms. Babcock moved to Bluffdale nearly

 1   13 years ago and was attracted to the charm of the City, open spaces, and large animal rights. They
 2   have enjoyed living in the community. They were pleased when they were able to purchase a one-
 3   acre lot two years earlier on which to build their home. They have not, however, been able to build
 4   on it due to personal and professional circumstances. In the meantime, they discovered that their
 5   lot is part of the CRO zone and large animals are not allowed.
 6
 7   According to Ms. Babcock’s research, there are 22 lots between Parry Farms and Spring View
 8   Farms that are 0.75-acre or above. One of the lots is 0.75-acre and the other is 0.96-acre. The other
 9   20 are all one-acre lots. She noted that all of the lots have been developed apart from the one
10   owned by her family.
11
12   Ms. Babcock requested an amendment to allow temporary structures as part of the CRO zoning
13   amendment for large animals on lots larger than 40,000 square feet. She explained that the CRO
14   zoning changes would only affect their residential acre lot as it is not yet built upon. She added
15   that this would avoid spot zoning circumstances. Mayor Timothy asked if the latest CRO zoning
16   changes affected properties in the zone.
17
18   City Planner/Economic Development Director, Grant Crowell stated that the Commission can
19   begin by going back to the ordinance and adding large animal rights to the CRO zone. He further
20   explained that in the R-1-43 zone the primary use of the lot is to be a residential home. Everything
21   else is accessory to the home. He stated that the bigger policy issue is to allow uses that are not a
22   residential home to become an allowed use in the R-1-43 zone.
23
24   In response to a question by Mayor Timothy, Ms. Babcock explained that they are building a
25   structure on the property and intend to make it their permanent residence at some point in the
26   future.
27
28   Mayor Timothy commented that the Commission recognizes individual circumstances. He asked
29   if a Temporary Special Use Permit could be issued. He felt there could be issues down the road
30   with accessory structures. He asked that Mr. Crowell draft a proposal.
31

13   4.     Presentation of the Police Semi-Annual Statistical Report, Staff Presenter, Police
14          Chief Andrew Burton.
15
16   Mayor Timothy reported that on a Friday at 4:00 a.m., Officer Garrett Carter impounded a vehicle
17   with two pounds of Methamphetamine worth $30,000 and arrested the owner. He presented
18   Officer Carter with a plaque from the City Council recognizing him for his service to the City of
19   Bluffdale.
20
21   Police Chief, Andrew Burton reported that the department’s community involvement remains
22   unchanged. He stated that they miss being able to do Old West Days, the Rodeo, Parade, and other
23   activities. Chief Burton reported that there will be a national night out during Old West Days this
24   year. He reported that they received a great deal of support from the neighborhoods.
25
26   Chief Burton reported that there is a New School Resource Officer at the Hidden Valley Middle
27   School. Officer Judson has been through the required two weeks of training to be a School
28   Resource Officer. He spends time at Bluffdale and Mountain Point Elementary Schools since they
29   are part of the Jordan School District.
30
31   Chief Burton reported that the department conducted a SWAT active shooter training at Mountain
32   Point Elementary. He noted that there are training and special events for officers to put their
33   escalation skills to use. There was a training in June on implicit bias and race relations led by
34   Professor Diane McAdams Jones from Utah Valley University.


                                                     10
                           DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                     BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                        MEETING MINUTES
                                     Wednesday, September 23, 2020

 1
 2   Chief Burton reported that the department also conducts Suicide Awareness Training and dealing
 3   with people who suffer from mental illness. He stated that in every circumstance they have to
 4   consider the safety of the officer and other persons.
 5
 6   In response to a question raised by Mayor Timothy, Chief Burton explained that in-house trainers,
 7   special counselors, and mental health specialists are hired periodically to conduct the training.
 8   Officers are encouraged to deescalate situations and pursue less-lethal options where possible. He
 9   noted that the Peace Officer Standards Training requires 40 hours of certification training per year.
10
11   Chief Burton reported that they hosted a live-fire pistol and rifle training qualification within the
12   department. He explained that there is also a class on de-escalation and using less-lethal options.
13   All supervisors within the department have bean bags to offer protection especially if a negotiation
14   situation with a suicidal person presents itself. He noted that the department also held a Public
15   Order Training in preparation for the ongoing protests.
16
17   Chief Burton reported that the department hosted an Awards Luncheon before COVID-19. He
18   stated that there had been less activity in 2020 as there was a reduction in traffic and calls for
19   service.
20
21   Chief Burton reported that the patrol officers remain on duty to address the COVID-19 situation.
22   Investigations personnel and support staff work remotely in an effort to minimize contact and stop
23   the spread of the Coronavirus. He noted that the department has taken every precaution. He noted
24   that two officers tested positive for COVID-19.
25
26   With regard to civic unrest, Chief Burton explained that the department has Inter-Agency and
27   Mutual Aid Agreements with other agencies in Salt Lake and Utah Counties. Bluffdale officers
28   have been deployed twice to other agencies during protests to help maintain public order.
29
30   In response to a question raised by Dave Kallas, Chief Burton explained that he had received
31   complaints that officers sent to other cities were not supported by City leadership. He explained



                                                       11
                            DRAFT – FOR DISCUSSION PURPOSES ONLY

                                      BLUFFDALE CITY COUNCIL
                                         MEETING MINUTES
                                      Wednesday, September 23, 2020

 1   that they were appreciated by the various agencies they offered help to fulfill their moral obligation
 2   to support the mutual aid concept.
 3
 4   Chief Burton reported that Utah County agencies established a public order unit of 40 officers
 5   when protests began in Saratoga Springs. There has also been an increase in mental subject and
 6   domestic violence calls. There has been a 25% decrease in traffic situations during the COVID-
 7   19 pandemic. Citations have also decreased because officers have less contact with the public.
 8
 9   Mayor Timothy complained about the vehicle noise and speeding along Redwood Road. Chief
10   Burton reported that the department received calls for service regarding the issue and they were
11   looking into the matter.
12
13   Chief Burton pointed out that the Police Department makes decisions regarding detaining,
14   arresting, or issuing citations based on facts, law, and safety to ensure that all are treated fairly. He
15   explained that the department uses body cameras for transparency. Chief Burton reported that
16   every use of force by an officer is reviewed by the Use of Force Review Bboard within the
17   department, which includes the Assistant Chief, Lieutenant Williams, and the sergeants of the non-
18   involved officer. In the event of injuries, the Review Board also includes the Internal Affairs
19   Investigator. Mayor Timothy expressed his appreciation to all of the officers for stepping up and
20   helping improve public awareness.
21

24   last year. He reported that since 2015 the Police Department has received 25 domestic violence
25   calls, 71 disturbance calls, 66 medical assistance calls, 19 child abuse calls, and 14 mental subject
26   related calls.

30   calls. The department received 29 calls in 2019 and 53 calls in 2020 from the Rockpoint
31   Apartments. He reported that there have been 16 vehicle theft calls from the Rockpoint Apartments
